Jordan relaxed instantly, leaning into Zoë's supportive embrace. He quickly pulled his Vanguard Pro onto his lap, his fingers flying across the keys.

<aac>Waffles, please,</aac> the Sydney voice of EchoType responded.

Standing a few feet away, Aubrie watched the interaction unfold. Her heart swelled. In any other dorm, in any other social circle, a girl watching another woman wrap her arm around the boy she was falling in love with would spark instant, bitter jealousy.

Aubrie felt nothing but pure, radiant joy. The pact was working. Jordan was being anchored, protected, and loved from multiple sides, exactly as they had designed.

They gathered their food and settled at a quiet table near the back of the hall. As Madison and Zoë debated the most efficient walking routes to the engineering buildings, Aubrie turned her full attention to Jordan.

"Okay, Astronomy 101," Aubrie began, her eyes sparkling with excitement. She knew his mother had forced him into the class to watch him fail, so she immediately set to work dismantling the trap.

"Don't worry about the math right now," Aubrie explained, leaning in close so he could hear her clearly. "The math is just the language we use to describe the system. Focus on the rules. A star is basically a giant balancing act. Gravity wants to crush it inward, and nuclear fusion wants to blow it apart. As long as those two forces are perfectly equal, the star survives. It’s a closed loop, Jordan. It’s entirely predictable."

Jordan watched her, captivated. His mother’s world was a chaotic, unpredictable minefield of shifting rules and sudden punishments. But here, Aubrie was offering him a universe governed by absolute, unbreakable logic.

He typed on his keyboard, hitting the execute key.

<aac>So... if you know the mass, you know exactly how it will die?</aac> the Australian voice asked.

"Exactly!" Aubrie beamed, practically vibrating with enthusiasm. "The rules never lie. If it's small, it cools into a white dwarf. If it's massive, it goes supernova. The universe doesn't play tricks, Jordan. We just have to learn how to read the patterns."

Jordan felt a slow, genuine smile spread across his face. For the first time in his life, he wasn't dreading the classroom. He was looking forward to it.

### Scene 2: Into the Stars

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 08:50 America/New_York

Outside the glass doors of Kincaid Hall, the sprawling campus was alive with a nervous, electric energy. Thousands of freshmen were crisscrossing the paved walkways, hunting for their first lecture halls. The suite family naturally began to splinter toward their respective academic buildings.

"Alright, duty calls," Madison sighed, adjusting the strap of her heavy backpack. She stepped forward and wrapped her arms around Jordan’s shoulders in a warm, lingering hug. "Have a great morning, J. See you this afternoon."

Zoë was next. She gave him a brisk but entirely sincere squeeze. "Do try to survive the chaos. We will reconvene at the suite this evening to analyze the day's data."

Jordan smiled, tapping his fingers against the armrest of his chair in a happy, staccato rhythm. As Madison and Zoë merged into the river of students heading toward the engineering quad, Aubrie stepped up beside him.

"Ready?" she asked, her eyes bright.

Jordan nodded, taking a deep breath.

Their 9:00 AM Astronomy 101 class was located in one of the older, brutalist concrete structures of the Science Complex. As they navigated through the heavy double doors and into the massive, tiered lecture hall, the architectural reality of the aging building immediately became apparent. The room was a steep amphitheater. A long flight of carpeted stairs led down to a stage at the very bottom, meaning the only wheelchair-accessible seating was a single row of desks bolted to the floor at the very top, in the absolute back of the room.

They settled into two seats near the exit. The room was already buzzing with the chatter of two hundred freshmen, and models of planets hung suspended from the high ceiling.

Jordan stared down at the massive projection screen at the front of the hall, his hands gripping the armrests of his chair. This was it. His mother’s weapon. The insurmountable mountain she had deliberately chosen for him, perfectly designed to ensure his academic failure.

He pulled his Vanguard Pro onto his lap, his fingers trembling slightly as he opened a blank document and typed. He angled the screen so Aubrie could read it without him having to trigger the Australian text-to-speech voice in the quiet room.

<aac>This is a lot,</aac> he typed, the cursor blinking rapidly.

"It's okay," Aubrie beamed, leaning in close so her shoulder brushed against his. Her excitement was a physical shield against his fear. "It's the real deal. No more high school physics. We're in this together, remember? I've got you. I'm not going to let you fail."

At exactly 9:00 AM, Dr. Alistair walked onto the stage far below them and turned on his microphone. A comfortable silence fell over the room as he brought up his first slide.

For Aubrie, the lecture hall instantly became a sanctuary—a place where the universe operated on clear, understandable laws. But for Jordan, the nightmare was just beginning.

From the very back row, the projection screen was nothing but a glowing, blurry white rectangle. The text on Dr. Alistair's slides smeared together into illegible gray smudges. Worse, the acoustics of the cavernous room were terrible. The professor’s voice echoed off the concrete walls, washing over Jordan in a muffled, distorted hum. He leaned forward, instinctively cupping his hand behind his ear, squinting so hard his eyes watered.

Panic spiked in his chest. *This was it.* His mother was right. He couldn't even perceive the class, let alone pass it.

Aubrie, hyper-observant as always, noticed his rigid posture immediately. She looked at his squinting eyes, then down at the way he was cupping his ear. The reality of his untreated sensory deficits became glaringly obvious.

She reached under the desk and gently, discreetly, took his hand.

Jordan flinched slightly at the unexpected contact, but her grip was firm, warm, and entirely certain. She laced her fingers through his and squeezed. With her free hand, she pulled her notebook directly between them and began writing in large, clear, block letters.

*I will take all the notes today. Do not panic.*

Jordan exhaled a shaky breath, staring at her neat handwriting. She quickly scribbled another line.

*You have that 1:00 PM meeting with Ms. Higgins at Educational Accessibility today. This is exactly what that meeting is for. Tell her you need front-row seating, a note-taker, and whatever else helps you see and hear. They HAVE to fix it.*

Jordan looked at her. In his mother's world, a barrier like this was a final judgment—proof that he was broken. In Aubrie's world, it was just an administrative error waiting for a logistical solution.

He squeezed her hand back, the terror in his chest slowly beginning to recede.

For the next fifty minutes, Dr. Alistair spoke about stellar nurseries and hydrostatic equilibrium. Jordan didn't understand the complex words, and he couldn't see the diagrams, but he stopped trying to force it. Instead, he just held Aubrie's hand and watched her. She was fascinated, hanging on every muffled word, her free hand flying across the notebook as she translated the muffled echoes of the professor into crisp, legible facts.

She was his safe person, and she loved this. Her excitement made the terrifying subject feel manageable. His mother's weapon suddenly seemed entirely defused. He had someone to climb the mountain with.

When Dr. Alistair finally dismissed the class, the spell was broken. The lecture hall erupted back into noise as students began packing their bags. Jordan let out a breath he felt like he'd been holding for nearly an hour. He had survived.

He looked at Aubrie as she finally let go of his hand to pack up her notebook.

"See?" she whispered directly into his ear, her smile bright. "Not so bad. And once Ms. Higgins gets your accommodations sorted, it'll be a breeze."

<aac>Not so bad,</aac> Jordan typed on his screen, offering her a small, genuine smile.

As they moved out into the crowded hallway, the mass of students jostled past them. Jordan tensed, his anxiety flaring at the claustrophobic press of bodies, but Aubrie seamlessly stepped in front of his wheelchair, acting as a small, sturdy buffer against the crowd.

"I guess we have to go our separate ways for our mid-morning classes," Aubrie said, her own expression dropping a little at the thought of leaving his side.

Jordan nodded, his heart sinking a fraction.

"Well," she said softly. And then, right there in the crowded hallway, she leaned down and wrapped her arms around his neck. This hug was different from the desperate, grounding embraces of the weekend. It was warm, lingering, and entirely voluntary—a reassuring physical promise that she wasn't disappearing.

He held onto her for a moment, absorbing her steadying presence. As she pulled back, her cheeks were slightly pink.

"Are you free for lunch?" she asked.

Jordan quickly pulled up his master schedule on his Vanguard Pro, though he already knew the answer. His heart gave a happy jolt.

<aac>Yes. At noon,</aac> EchoType replied in its crisp Australian cadence.

"Perfect," she beamed. "Meet me at the dining hall? Kincaid Commons?"

<aac>Yeah. Okay. Lunch.</aac>

"It's a date," Aubrie said brightly. Then her eyes went wide, realizing what she had just said. A deep, furious blush rushed all the way up to her hairline. "I mean... not a... you know. A literal date. Just... a lunch... with a time. I'll just see you then!"

She spun around, completely flustered, and hurried off toward the math building.

Jordan sat in the hallway, watching her go, a massive, uncontainable smile spreading across his face. He still had to face his mid-morning class entirely alone, a prospect that was undeniably intimidating. But as he maneuvered his wheelchair toward the elevators, his mind was remarkably at ease.

He had a schedule. He had a system. He knew he would see her on Mondays. Wednesdays, and Fridays. He knew he would see her in the dorm tonight. And now, he knew he would see her in just a couple of hours.

### Scene 3: The Ascent

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 10:15 America/New_York

"It's a date," Aubrie had said brightly in the crowded hallway of the Science Complex. Then her eyes had gone wide as she realized the weight of her own words. "I mean... not a... you know. A literal date. Just... a lunch... with a time. I'll just see you then!"

Flustered and blushing furiously, she had spun around to hurry off toward the math building. But after taking only two steps, she stopped. She turned back, marched right up to his wheelchair, leaned down, and quickly pressed her lips against his cheek.

It was a fleeting, lightning-fast peck, gone before Jordan could even register what was happening. She pulled away, her face burning an even deeper shade of crimson, and bolted down the corridor without looking back.

Jordan sat perfectly still in the middle of the rushing stream of freshmen. He slowly raised his hand, his fingertips resting against the skin of his cheek. The spot felt impossibly warm, practically glowing against the cool, air-conditioned air of the building.

It was his first kiss. From anyone. Ever.

His heart hammered a chaotic, frantic rhythm against his ribs. He felt entirely confused, completely overwhelmed, and wildly, desperately hopeful for noon to arrive.

But as the hallway began to empty out, reality slowly settled back in. For the very first time since arriving at CPI, the safety net of his suite family was entirely gone. Aubrie, Madison, Zoë, Lauren, and Hannah were all scattered across the massive campus. He was alone.

He pulled up his class schedule on his Vanguard Pro. *10:30 AM – History 101: Western Civilization.* It was located in Carter Hall, an older brick building situated near the upper quad.

Jordan took a deep breath, gripped the hand rims of his wheelchair, and pushed through the heavy glass doors back out into the humid Virginia morning.

The topography of the campus was notoriously unforgiving. CPI had been built into a series of rolling, aggressive hills, and the path to Carter Hall was a steep, winding ribbon of concrete. Jordan started up the first major incline, his arms straining with the effort. Without Aubrie or Lauren walking behind him to help push, the true, brutal weight of his own momentum settled squarely onto his shoulders.

Halfway up the first ridge, his triceps began to burn with a dull, throbbing ache. The wheelchair slowed to a crawl. He grit his teeth, forcing the wheels forward inch by inch, but gravity was pulling him backward just as relentlessly.

"Hey, man," a voice called out.

Jordan flinched, instinctively bracing himself for an insult or a sharp reprimand for blocking the walkway.

Instead, a tall guy wearing a faded CPI lacrosse hoodie stepped up behind him. "This hill is a beast. You want a hand?"

Jordan blinked, surprised by the easy, casual tone. He gave a short, grateful nod.

Without making a spectacle of it, the lacrosse player grabbed the push handles and easily rolled Jordan the rest of the way up the ridge, bringing them to the flat plateau near the library.

"Have a good one," the guy smiled, giving Jordan a quick pat on the shoulder before jogging off to his own class.

Ten minutes later, as Jordan approached the final, sweeping incline leading up to Carter Hall, his arms were practically shaking with exhaustion. The burn was intense, and his progress ground to a halt.

Once again, the universe intervened. A girl carrying a massive stack of art portfolios noticed him struggling. She didn't ask; she simply shifted her heavy folders to one arm, stepped behind his chair with the other, and leaned her weight against the handles to help him crest the final slope.

"They really should put an elevator on the outside of this hill," she joked, breathing heavily as they reached the top. She smiled warmly at him. "See you around."

As Jordan rolled toward the entrance of Carter Hall, a dark, familiar shadow crept into the back of his mind. *His mother's voice.*

He had needed help twice in the span of fifteen minutes just to get to a basic freshman lecture. His mother’s harsh words echoed in his head, sharp and undeniable: *You are a burden. You are fundamentally incapable. You don't belong here.*

For a terrifying moment, the anxiety threatened to swallow him whole. Maybe she was right. Maybe he should have just stayed home in Hampton Roads, hidden away in his bedroom where the ground was flat and the expectations were zero. If he couldn't even push himself to class, how was he supposed to survive four years here?

But then, he thought about the morning.

He thought about Madison stepping into the shower stall, taking the shampoo bottle from Aubrie with a bright, unquestioning smile. He thought about Zoë shielding him from the overwhelming noise of the cafeteria, gently reading the menu directly into his ear. He thought about Mark’s firm, respectful handshake.

And he thought about his cheek, which still felt impossibly warm.

They had seen every limitation he had. They had seen his absolute most vulnerable moments. And none of them had looked at him like he was a burden. They had looked at him like he was family. The random students on the pathway hadn't looked at him with pity, either—they had just seen a fellow student fighting a steep hill, and they had stepped in to help.

Jordan pushed open the door to Carter Hall and wheeled himself into the air-conditioned lobby. His arms ached, his chest was heaving, and he still had to figure out how he was going to hear the history professor.

But as he navigated toward the classroom door, a quiet, defiant thought blossomed in his mind.

*Maybe,* he thought to himself, *I'll just get used to the hills.*

### Scene 4: Western Civilization

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 10:30 America/New_York

The classroom in Carter Hall was much smaller and older than the massive science amphitheater, smelling faintly of lemon polish and old paper. Jordan wheeled himself into a spot at the end of the front row just as the professor—a tall, older man with a neatly trimmed beard—walked in and set his leather briefcase on the desk.

"Welcome to History 101," the professor announced, turning to the chalkboard to write his name. "I am Dr. Gable."

From his spot in the front row, Jordan immediately leaned forward. The white chalk on the dusty green board was completely unreadable, and Dr. face had been turned toward the wall when he spoke, muffling the sound. Jordan instinctively cupped his hand behind his ear and squinted, a familiar spike of anxiety hitting his chest.

Dr. Gable turned around. His eyes swept the front row, immediately landing on Jordan’s strained posture.

The professor didn't sigh. He didn't ask Jordan what was wrong, and he didn't draw the attention of the entire room to the boy in the wheelchair. Instead, he simply set the piece of white chalk down. He reached into his desk drawer, pulled out a thick, heavy-duty black marker, and walked over to the whiteboard mounted on the adjacent wall.

When Dr. Gable spoke again, he took two steps closer to Jordan's side of the room. He squared his shoulders, faced the class directly, and projected his voice with crisp, booming clarity that easily cut through Jordan's hearing deficit.

"Let's try the whiteboard instead," Dr. Gable boomed pleasantly. "Now, before we dive into Mesopotamia, I prefer to run this class as a discussion rather than a straight lecture. To make that work, I want everyone paired up. Can I get a volunteer to partner with our gentleman in the front row and help ensure he catches all the notes?"

A chair scraped against the linoleum a few rows back.

"I can do it, Professor."

Jordan’s head snapped up. The voice cut through the air with a distinct, undeniable cadence. He recognized the accent immediately—it was the exact same Sydney inflection that his EchoType software used.

A girl with dark hair pulled into a messy ponytail walked down the aisle and casually dropped her backpack into the empty desk right next to his wheelchair.

"Hey, mate," she smiled, extending her hand. "I'm Sarah."

Jordan blinked, slightly taken aback by her easy, radiant confidence. He shook her hand, finding her grip firm and friendly.

"I live over in Bennett Hall," Sarah continued, settling into her chair and pulling out a notebook. "Up on the fifth floor. You're the bloke from the second floor, right? The famous suite?"

Jordan tensed slightly, bracing himself for the inevitable invasive questions or the weird, pitying looks he usually got when people figured out his living situation.

But Sarah just smiled, completely unbothered. "Good on ya. I heard you've got the biggest bathroom in the building down there. Absolute luxury compared to the nightmare we're dealing with on five."

She made absolutely no big deal out of the fact that he was living with five women. To her, it was just another quirky piece of campus logistics. Her total lack of judgment made Jordan’s shoulders drop two inches in relief.

Jordan pulled open his Vanguard Pro. Because he didn't want to use his EchoType software and disrupt the quiet classroom with its digitized voice, he opted for a completely different approach. He opened a sleek, dark-themed terminal window and launched NeoEdit—a streamlined, text-based command-line interface that Zoë had pre-installed for him. It was simple, completely distraction-free, and most importantly, it allowed him to scale the neon-green text up massively against the stark black background so he could actually read it.

<aac>I'm Jordan,</aac> he typed, angling the screen so Sarah could see the large letters. <aac>Thank you for helping me.</aac>

"No worries at all, Jordan," Sarah whispered, her Australian accent warm and perfectly clear. "Let's smash this class."

The next hour was a revelation.

Dr. Gable was a masterful professor. He kept his voice loud, always making sure he was facing the front row when he spoke, and wrote his key points in massive, bold letters on the whiteboard. When the class shifted into a discussion about the agricultural revolution, Sarah proved to be the ultimate partner. She casually slid her notebook over so Jordan could read her meticulously organized bullet points.

When Dr. Gable posed a question to their side of the room, Jordan’s fingers flew across his keyboard.

<aac>The shift to agriculture created static borders, which required the invention of standing armies to defend them,</aac> Jordan typed into his terminal.

Sarah glanced at his screen, didn't miss a single beat, and raised her hand. "Jordan points out that the shift to agriculture created static borders," she announced clearly to the room, seamlessly acting as his voice. "Which required the invention of standing armies to defend them."

"Excellent point, Jordan," Dr. Gable nodded approvingly.

Jordan sat back in his chair, completely stunned. It was working. He was actively participating in a college-level discussion. He wasn't a burden, he wasn't a disruption, and he wasn't failing.

When the class finally ended, Jordan felt a rush of genuine, unadulterated triumph.

"Brilliant work today," Sarah smiled, packing her notebooks into her bag. She pulled out a pen and scribbled a string of numbers onto a scrap of paper, handing it to him. "Here’s my mobile number. Text me if you need the notes for whatever reason, or if you just want to go over the reading before Friday."

Jordan typed a quick <aac>Thank you</aac> into his terminal, taking the slip of paper with a massive smile.

"Catch you later, Jordan," Sarah waved, heading for the door.

As Jordan rolled out of Carter Hall and back into the bright Virginia sunshine, the brutal, rolling hills of the CPI campus didn't look nearly as terrifying as they had an hour ago. He had survived his first solo trek. He had found a professor who actually cared. He had made a new friend.

And most importantly, it was almost noon. It was time to go meet Aubrie.

### Scene 5: The Broken Elevator

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 12:00 America/New_York

As Dr. Gable formally dismissed the class, Sarah slung her backpack over her shoulder and looked down at Jordan.

"Where are you heading next?" she asked easily.

Jordan quickly typed into his terminal. <aac>Kincaid Commons. Lunch.</aac>

"Perfect, that’s exactly on the way to my next lecture," Sarah smiled. "I’m heading to the building right across from the lower delivery bays. You want a lift? These hills are absolute murder before noon."

Jordan hesitated for a fraction of a second before nodding. Sarah seamlessly stepped behind his wheelchair, grabbed the push handles, and steered him out of Carter Hall.

As they navigated the winding concrete paths downward, Jordan realized that his new friend was surprisingly strong. She handled the steep, downward grades with ease, holding the chair steady so gravity didn't rip it out of her control, chatting happily about the history syllabus the entire time. It was a massive relief not to have to fight the terrain, but more importantly, he was struck by how normal it felt. Sarah didn't act like she was doing a noble, charitable deed; she just saw a friend who needed to go in the same direction.

When they reached the bottom of the hill, they arrived at the rear, ground-level entrance of Kincaid Commons. The massive brick building was built directly into the incline, meaning the main entrance was a full story above them on the upper quad.

"Right, this is my stop," Sarah said, bringing the chair to a halt on the concrete patio just outside the delivery bays. She walked around to the front, offering a warm, polite smile and a quick wave. "Brilliant working with you today, Jordan. See you on Wednesday."

"Thank you," Jordan mouthed silently, offering a grateful wave back.

As Sarah jogged across the street toward her next class, a wave of anticipation washed over him. It was noon. He was finally going to see Aubrie.

He wheeled himself toward the glass doors of the lower level, his stomach tight with a mix of hunger and nervous excitement. But as he rolled into the dimly lit lower lobby, he found nothing but a series of loading docks and staff offices. There was no clear path to the main dining area above. He was completely lost.

His mPhone buzzed in his pocket. He pulled it out to see a text from Aubrie.

**Aubrie:** <sms>Hey! I’m here. Just inside the main entrance, by the stairs. Waiting for you! :)</sms>

Jordan’s heart sank. He was at the delivery entrance on the exact opposite side—and elevation—of the building. He quickly texted her back, his thumbs fumbling slightly on the screen.

**Jordan:** <sms>I think I'm at a different door. The lower level? I'm looking for the elevator but I don't see one.</sms>

He wheeled back and forth on the concrete patio, feeling a sudden, sharp jolt of panic. A cafeteria worker, pushing a large metal cart of supplies, noticed him spinning his wheels in the empty corridor.

"You looking for the way in, honey?" she asked kindly.

Jordan nodded quickly. He held up his mPhone, pointing to the word <aac>elevator</aac> on his screen.

The worker sighed, her face immediately falling into an expression of deep sympathy. "Ah, yeah, that's because the main public one is broken. The motor burned out this morning." She keyed her radio. "Hang on, let me get my manager. He can take you up the freight elevator."

A moment later, a man in a polo shirt with a manager's name tag pushed through a set of swinging metal doors. "Hi there," he said, assessing the situation. "Sorry about our elevator. Come with me, let's get you the scenic route."

He badged them through a heavy steel door marked *Staff Only.*

Jordan followed him, instantly crossing the threshold into a maze of loud, steamy industrial kitchens. The sensory assault was immediate and overwhelming. The clanging of massive metal pots, the aggressive hiss of industrial dishwashers, and the muffled, chaotic shouting of the line cooks hit him all at once. Because of his untreated hearing loss, the noise wasn't crisp; it was a terrifying, distorted wall of vibration and pressure. The heat of the ovens felt suffocating.

He hunched his shoulders, keeping his eyes glued to the manager's polo shirt, praying for it to end. The manager led him to a large, battered metal freight elevator and swiped a key against the panel. The heavy grated doors slammed shut, and they rode up one floor.

When the doors opened again, Jordan found himself emerging from an unmarked employee door directly into the bright, chaotic main dining area.

He saw her immediately.

Aubrie was standing right where she said she'd be, looking anxiously toward the front entrance. Her hair was catching the light from the massive glass windows, and she was shifting her weight from foot to foot, clearly worried about him.

Her face lit up the second she spotted him rolling out of the kitchen. But as she took a step forward, her brilliant smile faltered. She saw his expression.

Jordan stopped the wheelchair. The anxiety from the broken elevator, the claustrophobic sensory overload of the steamy kitchen, and the sheer frustration of being lost had all collided inside his chest. But underneath it all was a deeper, more profound terror.

Looking at Aubrie—radiant, safe, and waiting for him—a dark, invasive thought seized his mind. *What if this isn't real?*

The brief, terrifying moment of being lost, of being unable to find her, had felt entirely too much like his old life. He was suddenly terrified that this was all a beautifully constructed dream, a cruel trick of his own mind, and that he was going to wake up at any second, trapped back in his mother's apartment, forever at her mercy.

Aubrie saw the raw, unmistakable fright in his eyes. She didn't know the full logistical cause of his panic, only that the boy she was falling for was terrified.

She didn't hesitate. She rushed to him, closing the distance in seconds. Right there in the middle of the crowded dining hall, entirely uncaring of who was watching, she threw her arms around him and pulled him into a desperate, grounding hug. She buried her face in his neck, her arms serving as a firm, undeniable anchor against the overwhelming tide of his panic.

"Hey," she whispered into his ear, her voice fierce and absolute. "You're okay. You found me. You're safe."

She felt him trembling against her, his hands gripping the back of her shirt like it was a lifeline. Her heart ached for him. She realized something was deeply scaring him, something far heavier than just a broken elevator. She wished, with a sudden, powerful longing, that she could just skip her afternoon classes entirely and stay with him, acting as his shield for the rest of the day.

But she couldn't. They both had to keep moving forward.

The hug in the middle of the chaotic dining hall had grounded him. Jordan let out a shaky breath as Aubrie finally pulled back just enough to look into his eyes, though she kept a firm, reassuring hand on his shoulder.

She offered him a soft, affectionate smile, leaned in, and pressed a quick, gentle kiss to his cheek.

Jordan blinked, his breath hitching in his chest. It was only the second kiss of his entire life—the second one *today*—and the sudden, undeniable warmth of it chased away the last lingering chill of his panic.

"Come on," Aubrie said, her voice bright, deliberately ignoring the stares of a few passing upperclassmen. "Let's go get lunch. Together."

### Scene 6: The Taste of Freedom

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 12:15 America/New_York

Aubrie stepped behind his wheelchair and pushed him toward the long, winding serving lines. The noise and smell of the food were overwhelming, a chaotic mix of sizzling grease, baking bread, and a hundred overlapping conversations.

"Hey," Aubrie said, noticing him looking nervously at the stack of plastic trays. "It's a massive pain to try and balance a tray on your lap while rolling. Let me just grab yours for you. What do you want?"

Jordan looked up at the massive, illuminated menus above the stations. Pizza. Pasta. A deli. A grill. He froze. He was completely, utterly overwhelmed by the choices. His entire life, food had been a simple, non-negotiable command. His mother made something, and he ate it. If he didn't like what Aubrie Sinclair was serving, he didn't eat. He was never asked what he wanted.

"Jordan?" Aubrie asked gently, seeing him staring blankly at the glowing boards. "You okay?"

Then, he spotted it. At the grill station, a student in a maroon apron was flipping burgers on a flat top. A basket of fresh, golden french fries was resting right next to the fryer. His mother never made burgers, viewing them as greasy garbage, and fries were a rare, frozen-bag-in-the-oven affair that always came with a lecture about health.

He pulled out his mPhone, his thumbs moving quickly. He turned the screen toward her.

<aac>Can I... can I have that? A cheeseburger? And... fries?</aac>

"A burger? Heck yeah. Good choice," Aubrie said, not blinking an eye. She had absolutely no idea she had just enthusiastically approved two items his mother would have outright denied him. "What do you want on it?"

He was stunned. He typed a single word: <aac>Everything.</aac>

"You got it. One cheeseburger with everything, and a side of fries," she called to the worker over the glass partition. "What about a drink?"

He pointed to the soda fountains, another heavily restricted battleground he'd always lost at home. He typed: <aac>A soda?</aac>

"Perfect." Aubrie grabbed a plastic cup, filled it with cola, and placed it on the tray next to the paper boat of fries and the foil-wrapped burger. She then quickly grabbed her own lunch—a large salad and a bottle of water.

They arrived at the register. Jordan, suddenly realizing he had to actually purchase the food, began to panic. He fumbled awkwardly at his pocket, his fine motor skills failing him under the pressure as he tried to dig out his Poly Pass. He expected Aubrie to sigh in frustration or simply shove him aside and pay for it herself.

Instead, Aubrie set her tray down on the counter. She stepped to his side, gently placing her hand over his trembling fingers to steady them.

"Hey. Take your time. You've got this," she murmured softly. She helped him guide his wallet out of his pocket and slide the plastic Poly Pass free.

Jordan took a deep breath, reached up, and swiped his own card through the reader. The digital register chirped merrily, displaying a deduction from his tax-free **Forge Funds** balance. He had done it. He had chosen his own meal, and he had paid for it himself. It was a tiny, mundane transaction for anyone else, but for Jordan, it was a massive, intoxicating hit of pure independence.

Aubrie swiped her own card, balanced the two trays, and steered them toward a quiet table near the back windows overlooking the quad.

Once they were seated, Jordan unwrapped his burger. He took a bite, and the taste of salt, melted cheese, and absolute freedom flooded his senses. It was the best thing he had ever eaten.

Aubrie watched him with a soft smile, stabbing a cherry tomato with her plastic fork. "So? How was your mid-morning class? You survived the solo trek across the hills?"

Jordan’s face lit up. He swallowed, quickly wiping his hands on a napkin, and pulled his Vanguard Pro onto the table. He launched his sleek NeoEdit terminal, eager to tell her everything. He scaled the bright green text up so she could read it easily across the table.

<aac>The hills were brutal. But a couple of people actually helped push me. And the class was amazing. History 101.</aac>

"Yeah?" Aubrie leaned forward, genuinely invested. "Did the professor use the microphone? Could you hear anything?"

Jordan’s fingers flew across the keys.

<aac>I couldn't hear him at first, and he was writing on a dusty chalkboard. But he noticed me squinting. He stopped, switched to a whiteboard with heavy black markers, and moved closer so I could hear him. He didn't even make a big deal out of it.</aac>

"Jordan, that’s incredible!" Aubrie beamed, practically vibrating with second-hand excitement. "See? Some of the professors here actually get it."

<aac>But it gets better,</aac> Jordan typed, a massive grin on his face. <aac>He asked for a volunteer to partner with me. A girl sat next to me. She’s from Australia. Her name is Sarah.</aac>

Aubrie raised an eyebrow, smiling. "An Australian study buddy? That’s awesome."

<aac>She was great,</aac> Jordan continued. <aac>She lives on the fifth floor of Bennett Hall. I told her I was in the ADA suite, and she already knew about it. She didn't think it was weird at all. She took all my notes, and when I typed answers into my terminal, she read them out loud to the class for me. I actually participated.</aac>

Aubrie’s heart swelled. She reached across the table and lightly tapped the edge of his laptop. "Look at you," she said, her voice thick with pride. "Day one, and you're already leading classroom discussions and making friends on the fifth floor."

Jordan looked down at his keyboard, his cheeks flushing pink, but he couldn't stop smiling. The morning that had started with a terrifying astronomy lecture and a panic attack in the kitchen had completely turned around. He was capable. He belonged here.

Aubrie checked the clock on the dining hall wall. It was 12:45 PM.

"Eat up, genius," she said gently, packing up her empty salad container. "You've got a one o'clock appointment with Ms. Higgins at Educational Accessibility. It's time to make sure every single professor treats you like Dr. Gable did."

### Scene 7: The True Assessment

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 12:55 America/New_York

Aubrie pushed Jordan’s wheelchair across the sunlit quad toward Callahan Hall, home to the university's disability and accessibility offices. The building housed the Office of Educational Accessibility, and as they rolled through the heavy glass doors into the air-conditioned lobby, the reality of the clock finally caught up to them.

It was 12:55 PM. Aubrie had a 1:00 PM calculus lecture on the exact opposite side of campus.

She stopped the chair near the waiting area, her hands lingering on the push handles. She walked around to the front, her bright, energetic demeanor suddenly softening into quiet reluctance. She didn't want to leave him. After the panic in the kitchen and the triumph of the dining hall, stepping away felt wrong.

"Okay," Aubrie said softly, checking her watch. "I really have to run, or I'm going to fail calc before the syllabus is even handed out."

Jordan nodded, offering her a brave, reassuring smile. He pulled his Vanguard Pro onto his lap. <aac>Thank you for lunch,</aac> he typed in NeoEdit. <aac>Go to class.</aac>

Aubrie looked at him. She looked at the boy who had survived a terrifying morning, who had successfully navigated a college cafeteria, and who was now sitting tall in his chair, ready to face an administrator all by himself.

She leaned down, wrapping her arms securely around his neck, burying her face in his shoulder for a long, tight hug. When she pulled back, she kept her hands resting on his shoulders. She leaned in again, pressing a warm, lingering kiss against his cheek.

"Go to your meeting, Jordan," she whispered, her voice fierce and full of absolute certainty. "You got this."

She started to turn away, but paused. She looked back at him, her eyes shining with an emotion that was entirely unguarded and completely real.

"I love you," she said.

It wasn't a casual goodbye. It wasn't a joke. It was a firm, undeniable statement of fact.

Before Jordan could even process the words—before his brain could comprehend that someone had finally, genuinely said those three words to him and meant them—she was jogging backward toward the doors, a brilliant blush spreading across her cheeks. She gave him one last wave and disappeared out into the afternoon heat.

Jordan sat frozen in the lobby. His heart was hammering a chaotic rhythm against his ribs. *I love you.* The words echoed in his mind, wrapping around him like a physical shield.

"Jordan?"

He blinked, pulling himself out of his shock. Ms. Higgins was standing in the doorway of her office, a warm, welcoming smile on her face.

Jordan froze again, but this time for a completely different reason. She had called him *Jordan*.

He wheeled himself into her office, a massive wave of relief washing over him. Henry, the RA, had actually done it. The preferred name change they had entered into the CPI Student Portal on Friday night had cascaded through the university's administrative systems. To the professors and the staff, Aubrey Sinclair’s ghost was gone. He was officially Jordan.

Ms. Higgins closed the door behind them and took her seat at the desk. Jordan immediately opened his terminal.

<aac>Can my Poly Pass be changed to say Jordan Fuller?</aac> he typed, turning the screen toward her. <aac>Right now it says Aubrey Jordan Fuller.</aac>

Ms. Higgins read the bright green text and offered a sympathetic smile. "I'm so glad the preferred name system is working for your rosters, Jordan. Unfortunately, university policy mandates that the Poly Pass ID matches your legal government documentation for security and financial reasons. Until your name is legally changed in a courtroom, the ID has to bear your full legal name. But I promise you, in the eyes of this office and your professors, you are Jordan."

He nodded, accepting the bureaucratic reality. It was a small compromise for the massive victory of hearing his actual name out loud.

"Speaking of your Poly Pass, though," Ms. Higgins added, clicking into a campus security portal on her desktop monitor, "there *is* an important upgrade I can push to your card right now. As you've probably already discovered, CPI is built into steep mountain ridges. Because several of our residence halls—like Bennett Hall—are built directly into the hillsides with ground-level entrances on two different floors, the university lets anyone with a Poly Pass cut through those hillside dorms to use their elevators and skip climbing the hills during daytime hours, from 10:00 AM to 11:59 PM daily. From 12:00 AM to 9:59 AM, those buildings lock down so that you must be a resident of that hall or a signed-in guest escorted by whoever signed you in."

She typed a quick authorization code and hit enter. "Obviously, if you have an 8:00 AM or 9:00 AM class across campus, locking you out of a hillside elevator before 10:00 AM isn't acceptable. So Educational Accessibility is having Campus Access Control encode your Poly Pass for 24/7 pass-through access across every hillside residential building on campus. For any hillside dorm where you aren't a resident, your card will now unlock the exterior doors, the lobby, the accessible lobby restrooms, and partial elevator access 24/7—meaning when you tap your Poly Pass inside the elevator cab, it will only allow you to select the authorized floors that bridge the hill, such as the first and second floors in a building like Bennett Hall, while keeping the upper residential floors secure. And just like the handicap suites in all of our residence halls across campus, because you are registered with the Office of Educational Accessibility, your Poly Pass is encoded to automatically trigger the motorized door opener on *any* locked door you are authorized to access—holding standard campus doors open for five seconds, and dorm doors open for ten seconds, so you can roll through comfortably before they automatically close and lock behind you."

Jordan's eyes widened in appreciation as he nodded. That single credential update meant the steepest ridges on campus would never trap him, no matter how early his classes started or how late he studied.

Ms. Higgins leaned back, her sharp eyes scanning the sleek, matte-black Vanguard Pro resting on his lap. "I see you got a serious tech upgrade over the weekend. And it looks like this one actually holds a battery charge."

Jordan smiled, typing quickly. <aac>A friend gave it to me.</aac>

"Well, your friend is a lifesaver," Ms. Higgins said, her tone shifting slightly as she opened his sparse manila file. The polite, administrative mask she had worn on Thursday when his mother was hovering over him dropped away completely. Now, it was just the two of them, and she was entirely focused on the student sitting in front of her.

"Alright, Jordan. Let's have a real conversation," Ms. Higgins said, making sure she was facing him directly. "When you were in here last week, your mother submitted a medical file that only requested a wheelchair-accessible dorm and basic exam extensions. But sitting across from you, I noticed you struggling to see your screen, and cupping your ear to hear me. Tell me what happens in a lecture hall. When you look at the board from the back of the room, what do you see?"

Jordan frowned, slightly confused by the question. He opened his terminal.

<aac>I see what everyone else sees,</aac> he typed. <aac>Just colored blurs. You can't read the letters until you get to the front row. That's just how distance works.</aac>

Ms. Higgins stopped. Her pen hovered over her notepad. She stared at the bright green text on his screen, a cold realization washing over her.

"Jordan," she asked, her voice dropping to a very soft, careful register. "When you listen to a professor speak from across a large room... does it sound like they are underwater? Like a muffled hum?"

Jordan nodded, typing quickly. <aac>Yes. The echo makes the hum worse. That's why I cup my ear, to catch the sound waves better. Doesn't everyone?</aac>

Ms. Higgins leaned back in her chair. The sheer, horrifying scale of his mother's neglect suddenly snapped into focus. It wasn't just that Aubrie Sinclair had denied him medical care—she had actively normalized his suffering. She had kept his world so incredibly small that he had simply assumed his severely compromised perception was how the entire universe operated.

"Jordan," Ms. Higgins said gently, holding his gaze. "No. That isn't how distance works. Most people can read the board from the back row. Most people hear words clearly from across the room, without a hum. You aren't supposed to be struggling like this. And we are going to fix it right now."

She turned to her computer, her fingers flying across the keyboard. "I am authorizing an immediate, comprehensive override for your classroom logistics. Now, some of your classes, like History, are small enough that we can just move a desk to the front row. But that Astronomy lecture hall? There are no ramps to the floor. We physically cannot move you to the front row. So, we are going to bring the front row to you."

Jordan watched her, stunned, as she opened a locked cabinet behind her desk and pulled out a small black case and a sleek, silver tablet.

"First, the audio," Ms. Higgins said, opening the black case. "This is an FM Assistive Listening System. Dr. Alistair will be legally required to wear this lapel microphone during every lecture. You will plug a pair of over-ear headphones into this receiver box. His voice will bypass the room's acoustics and beam directly into your ears. It will sound like he is standing right next to you."

"Second, the visual," Ms. Higgins continued, tapping the silver tablet. "What operating system is your laptop running?"

Jordan typed: <aac>Aura Glade.</aac>

Ms. Higgins nodded knowingly. "Aura Glade is fantastic for stability, but it famously hates the university's proprietary screen-mirroring software. So, we bypass it. I am checking out this dedicated MagnaByte mPad to you for the semester. Dr. Alistair's projector will broadcast directly to this mPad in real-time, allowing you to zoom in on the slides from the back row. Furthermore, I am legally mandating that he email you high-resolution PDFs of all lecture materials twenty-four hours in advance."

"Third, your note-takers," Ms. Higgins said, a slight smile returning to her face. She pulled open a file drawer and extracted a thick stack of NCR-carbon-copy packets, sliding them across the desk.

"I am sending out an official email blast to all of your professors this afternoon to formally recruit peer note-takers for your remaining classes," she explained. "Usually, the professor makes an anonymous announcement. But since you already have Aubrie in Astronomy, and you mentioned your new friend Sarah in History, you can bypass the recruitment phase for those two entirely. Just hand these Authorization Forms directly to them."

She tapped the extra packets on the desk. "I'm giving you a few extra copies to keep in your bag, too. Just in case you make another friend in your afternoon classes who wants volunteer."

Jordan looked at the packets. <aac>Will they get in trouble for doing it?</aac> he typed quickly.

"The exact opposite," Ms. Higgins laughed. "Once they sign these forms and return them to my office, the university will formally compensate them. They will both be granted Priority Course Registration for next semester."

"However," Ms. Higgins said, her tone becoming gentle but firm. "My academic overrides only treat the symptoms. We need to address the root cause. You need actual medical support, Jordan. Glasses. Possible hearing aids. Real evaluations."

She pulled a referral slip from a stack on her desk and filled it out with a crisp, fluid motion.

"I am officially referring you to the CPI Student Health Center. They have an optometrist and an audiologist on staff. I want you to make an appointment this week. They will get the ball rolling to get you the actual hardware you need, completely independent of your mother's consent."

Jordan looked down at the slip of paper resting next to the FM receiver, the mPad, and the stack of note-taker forms. It was a golden ticket. He looked back up at Ms. Higgins, his fingers trembling slightly as he typed his response.

<aac>Thank you.</aac>

"Don't thank me yet, Jordan," Ms. Higgins smiled warmly, closing his file. "We're just getting started."

Ten minutes later, Jordan rolled out of Ms. Higgins's office and back into the sunlit lobby of Callahan Hall. His backpack was heavier now, but he felt lighter than he had his entire life.

He parked his wheelchair near the glass doors, pulled his mPhone from his pocket, and opened a new text message to Sarah.

**Jordan:** <sms>Educational Accessibility gave me an official note-taker form for Western Civ. If you sign it, the university gives you Priority Registration for next semester. Do you want it?</sms>

Less than thirty seconds later, his mPhone buzzed.

**Sarah:** <sms>Are you joking?! Priority registration is absolute gold! I would have done it for free anyway, mate! Absolutely YES. Bring it to class on Wednesday!!!</sms>

Jordan grinned, a massive wave of relief washing over him. He immediately opened Aubrie's contact thread.

**Jordan:** <sms>Ms. Higgins gave me the mPad and the microphone for Dr. Alistair. She also gave me a note-taker form for you. You get Priority Registration.</sms>

The reply from Aubrie came through almost instantly.

**Aubrie:** <sms>YES!!! Best news ever. Though I’d take your notes just to sit next to you anyway. 😊 Tell you all about Calc tonight! Go crush your afternoon classes! ❤️</sms>

Jordan stared at the little red heart emoji on his screen. He carefully locked his phone and slipped it back into his pocket. He wasn't navigating this campus alone. He had an entire constellation of people who enthusiastically wanted to be by his side.

He grabbed the hand rims of his wheelchair and pushed out into the afternoon heat, ready to tackle the hills.

### Scene 8: Afternoon Anchors

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 14:00 America/New_York

The afternoon sun was beating down on the brick walkways of CPI, but the steep inclines didn't seem nearly as daunting to Jordan anymore. Equipped with his new MagnaByte mPad, his FM receiver, and the stack of note-taker forms safely tucked into his backpack, he felt armed for the first time in his academic life.

His 2:00 PM class was Introduction to Psychology, located in one of the newer, sprawling social science buildings near the center of campus. The building was fully accessible, meaning Jordan easily navigated the automatic doors and took the wide elevator up to the third floor.

He rolled into the lecture hall ten minutes early. Unlike the massive astronomy amphitheater, this room was a wide, shallow horseshoe shape with designated accessible desks integrated right into the second row. He parked his chair at one of the wide wooden tables and began unpacking his gear.

He had just pulled his NeoEdit terminal up on his Vanguard Pro when a familiar, bright voice rang out.

"Well, look who it is!"

Jordan spun around. Madison was walking down the tiered steps, a massive iced coffee in one hand and her backpack slung over her shoulder. Her face lit up with a brilliant smile as she spotted him.

Jordan’s face mirrored her excitement. He furiously typed into his terminal: <aac>You're in this class?</aac>

"Psych 101? Absolutely," Madison said, dropping her bag onto the chair right next to him. She slid into the seat, perfectly comfortable invading his space. "It fulfills one of my general education requirements for engineering. Plus, it's fascinating."

She glanced at his desk, noticing the sleek silver tablet and the black FM receiver box he had just set down. "Whoa. New toys? How did the meeting with Ms. Higgins go?"

Jordan grinned. He reached into his backpack, pulled out one of the NCR-carbon-copy packets Ms. Higgins had given him, and slid it across the desk toward Madison.

<aac>Ms. Higgins gave me the tech to mirror the projector and hear the professor directly,</aac> he typed, turning the screen so she could read it. <aac>And she gave me these. If you sign this form to be my official note-taker, the university will give you Priority Course Registration for next semester.</aac>

Madison’s blue eyes went completely wide. She stared at the packet, then looked up at him. "Are you serious? Priority Registration? Jordan, do you have any idea how hard it is to get into upper-level engineering labs as a sophomore? People literally cry over scheduling."

Jordan nodded, typing quickly: <aac>Do you want it?</aac>

"Do I want it? Jordan, I was going to sit next to you and copy my notes for you anyway!" Madison laughed, her bright energy practically vibrating in the quiet room. She immediately dug a pen out of her bag and started filling out the top sheet. "You just saved my entire spring semester. You are officially my favorite person on this campus."

As Madison enthusiastically filled out the paperwork, the quiet, safe intimacy of their shared suite bubble settled over the desk. Jordan watched her write, feeling a sudden, overwhelming urge to share the impossible thing that had happened just before his meeting.

He took a deep breath, his fingers hovering over his keyboard.

<aac>Aubrie dropped me off for the meeting,</aac> he typed, hesitating for a fraction of a second before committing the rest of the sentence to the screen. <aac>She kissed my cheek. And she told me she loved me.</aac>

Madison stopped writing. She put her pen down and looked at the bright green text on his screen.

For a terrifying, split second, Jordan worried he had made a mistake. In any normal social dynamic, telling a girl who explicitly fancied you that her roommate had just confessed her love would result in immediate jealousy, drama, or a hasty retreat.

Instead, Madison’s face broke into a massive, radiant beam. Her eyes softened with genuine, unadulterated joy.

"She did?" Madison whispered, leaning in close so only he could hear. "Oh, Jordan. That's incredible."

She reached out and placed her hand gently over his. There was no trace of bitterness or competition in her touch. It was pure, supportive warmth.

"I told you guys yesterday afternoon," Madison said softly, her thumb lightly tracing the back of his hand. "Aubrie and I talked about this. We don't want to compete for you. We just want to love you. The Constellation is working, J. It's really working."

Jordan stared at her, his heart swelling so much it felt like it might crack his ribs.

<aac>You're really okay with it?</aac> he typed, his hands shaking slightly.

"I am completely, one hundred percent okay with it," Madison promised, her gaze steady and true. She squeezed his hand before pulling back to finish signing the note-taker form. "Because I love you, too, Jordan. And since Aubrie is okay with that... everything is going to be just fine."

The psychology professor walked into the room, signaling the start of the lecture. Madison quickly passed the signed carbon copy back to him and opened her own notebook, ready to work.

Jordan carefully folded the form and tucked it into his bag. He plugged his headphones into the FM receiver and looked at the MagnaByte mPad, which had just lit up with the professor's first slide.

He was sitting in a college classroom. He could see the notes. He could hear the lecture. And he was sitting next to a brilliant, beautiful girl who had just promised him that she loved him, too, without any of the messy, territorial conflict that usually destroyed relationships.

His mother's weapon had been completely dismantled. The world wasn't a terrifying, isolating prison anymore. It was a wide-open universe, and his Constellation was shining brighter than he had ever thought possible.

### Scene 9: The Girls of 505

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 15:30 America/New_York

The heavy wooden door to Room 505 swung open, and Sarah Braithwaite breezed into the dorm, dropping her backpack onto her desk with a loud, triumphant thud.

Her two roommates were already there, escaping the humid Virginia afternoon. Jessica, her fellow Australian from the deep Outback, was sprawled across her bed with a massive biology textbook propped against her knees. Their third roommate, Chloe from Richmond, was sitting at her desk, painting her nails a bright CPI maroon.

"Alright, ladies," Sarah announced, a massive grin spreading across her face. "You are looking at a girl who just secured Priority Course Registration for the spring semester."

Chloe looked up, nearly smudging her thumb. "Wait, seriously? How? We're freshmen. The registrar basically treats us like peasants."

"I volunteered to be a peer note-taker in my Western Civ class," Sarah said, kicking off her shoes and falling backward onto her own bed. "But that isn't even the best part. You know the massive rumor going around the building? The clerical error on the second floor?"

Jessica peeked over the top of her biology book. "The bloke in the wheelchair who got assigned to the suite full of girls? Room 214?"

"That's the one," Sarah nodded, her eyes bright with excitement. "His name is Jordan. He's in my History class, and I’m his official note-taker. Honestly, he is an absolute ripper."

Chloe capped her nail polish, fully invested in the gossip now. "Okay, give us the details. Is it as weird as everyone is saying? What's he like?"

"He's not weird at all," Sarah defended immediately. "He's actually incredibly sweet. He doesn't talk out loud—he types everything into this sleek terminal program on his laptop, and I read it for him in class. But he's just... so polite. And smart. He completely saved my skin when Dr. Gable started grilling our side of the room about the agricultural revolution."

Sarah stared up at the ceiling tiles, a softer, more thoughtful expression crossing her face.

"It was actually really refreshing," she admitted quietly. "He's just so calm. A real top bloke." She let out a small sigh. "He seems so much nicer than Tom, to be honest."

Jessica raised an eyebrow. "Trouble in paradise with the lacrosse king?"

"Not trouble, exactly," Sarah said, waving a hand dismissively, though a flicker of frustration crossed her features. "Tom's just been so aggro lately. Everything is a competition, or he's stressing about his starting spot on the team, or he's getting possessive about my schedule. It’s exhausting. Sitting next to Jordan today... there was no ego. No posturing. Just a really gentle, decent guy trying to get through the day."

Chloe grinned, leaning back in her chair. "Uh-oh. Sounds like someone is getting a little crush on the boy from 214."

"Oh, shut up," Sarah laughed, throwing a pillow at Chloe’s head. Chloe dodged it easily. "Absolutely not. Besides, even if I was looking, he is entirely off the market."

Jessica sat up, closing her biology book over her finger to keep her page. "Wait, is the other half of the rumor true? Are they actually hooking up in that suite?"

"I don't know about hooking up, but he is deadset gone for one of his roommates," Sarah said, her smile returning. "Her name is Aubrie. When class ended, I walked him down the hill to Kincaid Commons. The second he typed that he was meeting her for lunch, his whole face completely lit up. He is absolutely smitten with her. It’s actually bloody adorable."

Chloe sighed dramatically, resting her chin on her hands. "A sweet, smart guy who looks at his girl like that? Aubrie is living the dream. Meanwhile, I can't even get a text back from the guy in my chem lab."

"Right?" Sarah laughed, sitting up and pulling her laptop out of her bag to start on her reading before Wednesday. Right now, she was just happy she had made a new friend in Western Civ, and that the boy from Room 214 had finally found someone who made him smile like that.

### Scene 10: Evening Data

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 20:00 America/New_York

The first official day of classes had been a whirlwind. By eight o'clock that evening, the suite had gathered in Room 216 to decompress.

It was Jordan’s first time crossing the threshold into the other half of their suite, and it required careful maneuvering. Because Bennett Hall was a traditional 1960s cinderblock dorm, the room was incredibly small—originally built for two students, but now crammed with three. It held a single Twin XL bed, a heavy wooden bunk bed, two standard-issue desks, and two dressers. Floor space was at an absolute premium, and Jordan’s wheelchair took up a good chunk of the narrow center aisle.

But no one minded the tight squeeze. The cramped quarters just forced a comfortable, domestic closeness. Aubrie was perched on the edge of the bottom bunk, her shoulder brushing comfortably against Jordan’s arm. Madison was sitting cross-legged on the floor, leaning her back against one of the dressers. Zoë and Hannah were crammed together on the single Twin XL bed, sharing a bag of pretzels with Hannah’s boyfriend, Mark, who was leaning casually against the adjacent desk chair.

Up on the top bunk, her back pressed against the cool cinderblock wall to carve out a tiny pocket of privacy, Lauren Barnes was on a video call with her boyfriend, Tom.

"...and my last lecture finished late, so I had to sprint to Kincaid Commons before the grill closed," Lauren finished, keeping her voice low so she didn't disrupt the laughter echoing in the small room below her. "It was a crazy day. How was yours?"

"Boring without you," Tom said through the phone speakers. He was sitting in his own dorm room, tossing a lacrosse ball up and catching it. "So, how are the roommates? Not driving you crazy yet?"

"Never! They're the best," Lauren smiled, looking down over the edge of the mattress. Aubrie was playfully nudging Jordan's shoulder as he showed her something on his screen. "Actually, the most unbelievable thing happened. You're not going to believe it. One of our suitemates... is a guy."

She said it with a light laugh, expecting Tom to share in the sheer, bizarre comedy of the situation.

But on the screen, Tom’s smile didn't just fade—it tightened. He stopped tossing the lacrosse ball. "A guy? What are you talking about, Lauren?"

The playful warmth in his voice vanished instantly, replaced by a sharp, suspicious edge that caught Lauren completely off guard.

"Housing made a huge mistake with his name," Lauren explained quickly, her stomach giving a weird, unexpected flutter of anxiety. "His legal name is Aubrey, but he goes by Jordan. They accidentally assigned him to our suite."

"So they moved him, right?" Tom interrupted, his tone suddenly flat and demanding.

"Well, no," Lauren said, trying to keep her voice light. "He's in a wheelchair, and we have the only accessible bathroom on the floor. And honestly, Tom, he is incredibly sweet. He and Aubrie just clicked instantly. We all just decided to make it work."

Tom stared at her through the screen. His eyes darted slightly, his jaw clenching as his mind immediately jumped to the most toxic, cynical conclusion possible.

"Right. A guy living with five girls," Tom scoffed quietly, shaking his head. "You know exactly what he's doing, Lauren. Don't play dumb."

Lauren blinked, recoiling slightly against the cinderblock wall. "What? Tom, he's in a wheelchair. He's totally innocent."

"I know how guys operate. You think I'm an idiot?" Tom sneered, his voice dropping to a harsh whisper. "One guy, five girls, locked in a suite together? He's playing the pity card to get you all into bed. He's probably trying to work his way through the whole room."

"Tom, that is disgusting!" Lauren hissed, horrified by the accusation. "He's not like that at all! Aubrie loves him, and he loves her. It's completely wholesome. Even Mark—Hannah's boyfriend—hangs out here all the time. Mark knows exactly what the living arrangement is, and he's totally cool with it! He thinks Jordan is a great guy!"

"Then Mark is a naive idiot if he's letting some random guy sleep a few feet away from his girlfriend," Tom snapped defensively.

Because the room was so small, and because Tom's voice had spiked with defensive anger, the sharp, tinny audio easily bled through the phone's tiny speaker. Down by the desk, Mark paused mid-chew, a pretzel halfway to his mouth. He looked up at the top bunk, his eyebrows raised in utter disbelief.

"I'm not Mark," Tom continued, completely oblivious to his audience. "Look, my roommate just got back with pizza. I gotta go. Just... keep your distance from the guy, alright? Don't get sucked into whatever weird, messed-up thing they've got going on over there."

"It's not messed up—"

"I'll call you tomorrow," Tom cut her off. "Bye."

The screen went black.

Lauren sat on the top bunk, staring at her reflection in the dark glass of her mPhone. A cold, heavy stone of unease settled perfectly in the center of her chest. It wasn't just that they had disagreed. It was the sheer, absolute certainty in Tom's voice as he accused Jordan of something so vile.

The quiet chatter in the room below had paused.

"Well," Mark said dryly, tossing his pretzel back into the bag. "I guess I need to find a new study partner for PolySci. Because there is absolutely no way I'm sitting next to that guy every Monday, Wednesday, and Friday."

Hannah rested her head on Mark's shoulder, shooting Lauren a sympathetic look. "I'm so sorry, Lauren. He's completely out of line."

Lauren hugged her knees a little tighter, her gut screaming at her that Tom was dangerous. The way he had so easily twisted a beautiful, supportive friendship into something dirty made her feel sick to her stomach.

"Hey, Lauren!"

Lauren jumped slightly, looking over the railing of the bunk bed.

Down in the narrow aisle, Jordan had angled his wheelchair so he could look up at her. His sleek Vanguard Pro was resting on his lap. He typed quickly, and the neon-green text on his NeoEdit terminal expanded so she could read it clearly from the top bunk.

<aac>Madison said you like Dark Aero aesthetics. Look what Zoë just did to my desktop.</aac>

Lauren forced a smile, grateful for the distraction, and carefully climbed down the wooden ladder. She squeezed into the space between Jordan's chair and the desk to look at his screen.

Zoë had managed to skin Jordan's Aura Glade window manager with a flawless, glossy, early-2000s Dark Aero theme—complete with deep glass transparencies, dark metallic taskbars, and glowing neon-blue accents. But sitting right in the center of the glossy desktop, completely untouched, was his NeoEdit terminal window, still rocking the classic, retro 1980s black-and-green aesthetic.

Jordan kept typing. <aac>The darker colors actually really help cut the glare. It's so much easier on my eyes when everything isn't bright white.</aac>

"Oh, wow," Lauren said, her smile becoming genuine as she looked at the sleek interface. "That looks incredible, Jordan. And if it helps you see better, that's even more perfect."

Jordan smiled, turning to his backpack hanging off the back of his chair. He pulled out the FM receiver box and the silver mPad Ms. Higgins had given him, but he didn't stop there. He reached into the front pocket and pulled out two crisp, official-looking referral slips from Educational Accessibility, handing them to Aubrie.

<aac>Ms. Higgins gave me these, too,</aac> he typed, turning the screen so the whole room could see. <aac>Referrals to the Student Health Center. For a real optometrist and an audiologist. They're going to get me glasses and see about hearing aids.</aac>

A collective gasp of excitement swept through the tiny room. Aubrie threw her arms around his neck, burying her face in his shoulder, while Madison reached up from the floor to high-five him. Even Mark leaned over to clap him on the shoulder. It was a massive, tangible step toward independence, entirely outside his mother's control.

Lauren sat down on the edge of the bottom bunk, her shoulder pressing against Aubrie's as she watched the celebration unfold. She looked at Jordan, who was grinning ear to ear as Hannah examined the FM receiver. He wasn't a predator. He wasn't playing a game. He was just a kind, resilient boy finally getting the medical care he deserved, surrounded by people who genuinely cared about him.

Lauren looked back down at her dark phone resting on the desk. The contrast between the cold, toxic delusion of her boyfriend and the absolute, wholesome safety of Room 216 was jarring.

### Scene 11: The Open Doors

**Date:** 2014-08-25 at 21:15 America/New_York

By quarter past nine, the comfortable exhaustion of the first day of classes had finally settled over the cramped room. It was time to break up the gathering so everyone could actually get some sleep.

Mark stood up, stretching his arms toward the low cinderblock ceiling. "Alright, ladies, and Jordan. I've got an 8:00 AM lab tomorrow, so I need to head up to the third floor."

He leaned down, wrapping Hannah in a warm, lingering hug and kissing her softly. Then, he turned to Lauren, who was still sitting on the edge of the bottom bunk, looking physically drained from the emotional whiplash of Tom's phone call.

Mark stepped over and pulled Lauren into a solid, brotherly hug. "Hey," he said softly, making sure she was looking at him when he pulled back. "You don't have to put up with that garbage. If you decide you don't want to see him anymore, you let us know. This whole room has your back. All of us."

As Mark said it, he made sure to meet Hannah’s eyes across the room. Hannah just offered a warm, supportive smile. There was absolutely zero jealousy, no possessive anxiety, no drama. She knew exactly who her boyfriend was—a good guy looking out for her roommate. It was the exact opposite of the toxic, paranoid reality Tom lived in.

"Thank you, Mark," Lauren whispered, managing a small, genuine smile.

"Anytime," Mark grinned, giving Jordan a fist bump on his way out the door.

With Mark gone, Jordan locked the wheels of his Vanguard Pro. He reached out, and one by one, Hannah, Zoë, and Lauren stepped into the narrow aisle to give him a hug goodnight. The physical touch, which just a few days ago would have sent him into a spiral of sensory panic, now just felt like an anchor. He squeezed Lauren back tightly, wanting her to know that Mark was right—they were all on her side.

"Okay, team," Madison said, standing up and brushing off her pajama pants. "We're heading back to 214."

Before Madison pushed Jordan’s chair toward the connecting bathroom door, Aubrie looked back at the girls of 216.

"We're leaving both the inner bathroom doors open tonight," Aubrie announced, her tone leaving no room for argument. "Unless someone is actively using the toilet, we prop them open. Lauren, if you can't sleep, or if you just need to talk, or if you get scared... you just yell. We are ten feet away. Got it?"

Lauren felt a lump form in her throat. She nodded, pulling her knees to her chest. "Got it. Thank you."

Madison wheeled Jordan into the connecting bathroom, and Aubrie followed, clicking the door to 216 mostly shut, but leaving a deliberate, welcoming crack.

The bathroom routine had quickly become a seamless, integrated part of their evening. There was no awkwardness, no hesitation. They took turns using the toilet, and when it was Jordan's turn, Aubrie and Madison worked together to help him. They treated his physical vulnerability with the same casual, loving care they applied to everything else. They helped him manage his clothing, stabilized him during the transfers, and made sure he was comfortable. For Jordan, the deep, burning shame his mother had instilled in him about his bodily needs was slowly being washed away by their gentle, practical support.

Once they were back in Room 214, they left the bathroom door wide open, as promised.

Madison and Aubrie easily helped Jordan change out of his daytime clothes and into his soft cotton pajamas, then quickly changed into their own sleepwear.

As Aubrie pulled her hair up into a messy bun, she turned to Jordan, who was sitting in his wheelchair at the foot of the beds.

"Alright, J," Aubrie said softly, her eyes twinkling. "Who do you want tonight?"

Jordan froze. He looked back and forth between the two beautiful girls standing in the dim light of the dorm room. His brain practically short-circuited as it tried to process three simultaneous, impossible realities.

First, he still couldn't entirely believe that Aubrie Kelly—bright, energetic, brilliant Aubrie—had willingly spent the last two nights squeezed into a bed with him. Second, the fact that he was actually being <aac>asked</aac> to choose between them felt like a fever dream. And third, he knew with absolute certainty that if he typed, <aac>I just want to sleep alone tonight</aac>, neither of them would be offended. They would just help him into bed and kiss his forehead. The Twin XL mattress was tiny, and he had the complete autonomy to say no.

But he didn't want to say no.

Slowly, Jordan turned his head and looked directly at Madison.

He braced himself, terrified that Aubrie's feelings would be hurt. But when he glanced back at her, Aubrie was just smiling. Her face was entirely relaxed, practically glowing with affection for both of them. The Constellation was working exactly as they had designed it. There was no competition.

Madison’s face lit up with a brilliant, breathless smile. "Really?" she whispered.

Jordan nodded, a shy, hopeful smile pulling at his lips.

Working together, the two girls helped Jordan navigate the difficult transfer from his wheelchair to the mattress of Bed A. Once he was settled, he used his arms to physically drag his legs and torso all the way over to the far edge, pressing his back flush against the cool cinderblock wall to give Madison as much space as possible.

Madison climbed into the bed next to him. This was her first time in the bed with him, and Jordan’s very first time having a girl other than Aubrie under the covers.

Instead of turning her back to the room, Madison settled on her side, facing away from him. Jordan hesitated for a second, then gently slid his arm around her waist, pulling himself flush against her back. He was the big spoon. It was the first time in his life he had ever held someone like this, acting as the protector, the anchor, the embrace.

Madison let out a soft, contented sigh, reaching down to weave her fingers perfectly through his where his hand rested against her stomach.

"Goodnight, Jordan," Madison whispered, her voice incredibly soft in the quiet room.

Aubrie walked over to the wall switch. "Goodnight, you two," she smiled. She flicked off the overhead light, plunging the room into darkness, and climbed into Bed B.

Jordan lay there in the quiet dark, feeling the steady rise and fall of Madison's breathing against his chest. For the first time since these five girls had crashed into his lonely, isolated life on Saturday morning, the tiny, terrified voice in the back of his head—the voice that sounded exactly like his mother, whispering that this was all an elaborate, cruel joke—was finally silent.

He closed his eyes, pulling Madison just a fraction of an inch closer. He was safe. He was loved. And he was finally, completely at peace.

## The Anatomy of Trust

### Scene 1: The Mega-Meetup

**Date:** 2014-08-26 at 09:45 America/New_York

The massive, stadium-style lecture hall in the College of Health Sciences was already buzzing with the low, chaotic energy of three hundred freshmen. The room was steeply tiered, built to accommodate massive general education requirements, with a wide, flat accessible row running straight across the middle tier.

Aubrie pushed Jordan’s Vanguard Pro through the double doors, followed closely by Madison, Zoë, Lauren, Hannah, and Mark. They claimed a massive block of seating right in the center of the accessible row. Jordan parked his wheelchair at the end of the long desk, plugging his headphones into the FM receiver and setting up his new MagnaByte mPad next to his laptop.

The fact that all seven of them were in this exact section was a massive, statistically absurd coincidence. But the fact that Jordan was in the class at all was a masterstroke of administrative negligence by his mother.

Like all undergraduates at CPI, Jordan was required to complete two credits of Health or Physical Education to graduate. Over the summer, Aubrie Sinclair had logged into his student portal to build his schedule. She had seen "HSC 201" fulfilled the requirement, checked the box, and moved on, completely failing to read the actual course title or description.

If she had bothered to look, she would have realized she had just enrolled her heavily sheltered, medically isolated son in *Dimensions of Human Sexuality*.

For eighteen years, Aubrie Sinclair had religiously signed the opt-out forms for every single public school Family Life and sexual education module. Now, thanks to her own careless rushing, the university was about to give him a comprehensive, college-level education on the exact subject she had spent his entire life trying to hide from him.

"Jordan!"

A bright, distinctly Australian voice cut through the chatter. Jordan looked up from his terminal. Sarah Braithwaite was bounding down the tiered stairs, a massive smile on her face. Two other girls were trailing closely behind her.

Jordan’s face lit up. He quickly typed into his NeoEdit terminal, the neon-green text glowing against the sleek, dark glass of his new Dark Aero window manager.

<aac>Sarah! You're in this class too?</aac>

"Gen-ed requirement," Sarah grinned, dropping her backpack onto the desk next to Lauren. "I saw you guys roll in. These are my roommates from the fifth floor. Jessica, and Chloe."

Jessica, who shared Sarah's accent but carried herself with a much more grounded, no-nonsense energy, offered a friendly wave. "Nice to finally meet the famous Room 214."

Chloe smiled warmly, claiming a seat next to Madison. "Sarah hasn't stopped talking about how much you saved her in Western Civ yesterday."

Sarah laughed, sliding into the seat between Jessica and Lauren. She turned to Lauren, offering her hand. "I'm Sarah, by the way. I don't think we formally met in the lobby on Saturday."

"Lauren," she replied, shaking Sarah's hand with a bright, genuine smile. "I'm in 216. It is so nice to meet you. We were just saying how incredibly lucky Jordan was to get you as a note-taker on the very first day."

"Oh, please, he's the one who got me Priority Registration," Sarah said easily as she pulled out her notebook. "He's an absolute lifesaver. How's the whole suite dynamic going? Is it as chaotic as everyone thinks?"

"Honestly? It's amazing," Lauren admitted, her smile softening as she looked down the row at Jordan, who was currently showing Jessica how his FM receiver worked. "It just... works. We all really look out for each other."

They fell into an easy, comfortable conversation. Sitting side-by-side in the crowded lecture hall, the girls bonded instantly over the shared anomaly of Jordan’s living situation and the chaotic reality of freshman year. Neither of them brought up their boyfriends. In the bright, bustling energy of the morning, surrounded by friends, the dark, uneasy shadow of Lauren's phone call with Tom the night before felt temporarily pushed away.

At exactly 10:00 AM, the lights in the auditorium dimmed slightly. A tall, fiercely confident professor with a wireless microphone clipped to her lapel strode to the center of the stage. She didn't look like a stuffy academic; she exuded a vibrant, no-nonsense energy that instantly commanded the room.
